Feynman Integrals of Grassmannians
Tai-Fu Feng a,b,c,d , Hai-Bin Zhang a,b,c , Chao-Hsi Chang e,f,g

Abstract
We embed Feynman integrals in the subvarieties of Grassmannians through homogenization of the integrands in projective space, then obtain GKZ-systems satisfied by those scalar integrals. The Feynman integral can be written as linear combinations of the hypergeometric functions of a fundamental solution system in neighborhoods of regular singularities of the GKZ-system, whose linear combination coefficients are determined by the integral on an ordinary point or some regular singularities. Taking some Feynman diagrams as examples, we elucidate in detail how to obtain the fundamental solution systems of Feynman integrals in neighborhoods of regular singularities. Furthermore we also present the parametric representations of Feynman integrals of the 2-loop self-energy diagrams which are convenient to embed in the subvarieties of Grassmannians.



pacs
02.30.Jr, 11.10.-z, 12.38.Bx

I
Introduction

Although the discovery of Higgs with mass around
125\;{\rm GeV}

CMS2012
;
ATLAS2012
indicates
a great success of the Standard Model (SM), the updated experimental
data on the anomalous magnetic moment (MDM) of muon
ADM2021
and mass of the charged
gauge boson
Wmass
seem to herald the signals of new physics beyond.
Before the collision energy of the center of mass of the running collider
reaches the threshold energy of new physics, the SM theoretical
predictions of various physical observations should be accurately calculated
Heinrich2021
.
Through comparison between the experimental data and corresponding SM evaluations,
the energy scale of new physics and strength of the coupling of new physics
particles to fields of the SM can be roughly estimated.

To obtain the SM evaluations of physical observables accurately,
one has to compute the scalar integrals of corresponding Feynman diagrams
involved at first, which is a challenging work in radiative corrections in quantum field theory.
In dimensional regularization, any scalar integral can be expanded as a Laurent series
around
\varepsilon=0
where the time-space dimension
D=4-2\varepsilon
.
For example, the Feynman integral of the 1-loop triangle diagram
C_{{}_{0}}
is expanded as

\displaystyle C_{{}_{0}}=C_{{}_{0}}^{(0)}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}C_{{}_{0}}^{(i)}\varepsilon^{i}\;,

(1)

around
D=4
, and the well-known result of
C_{{}_{0}}^{(0)}
is presented in the
literature
tHooft1979
. However, evaluating
C_{{}_{0}}^{(i)}

precisely is also necessary to obtain high order radiative corrections
to the amplitude self-consistently when the virtual interactions
originate from the counter terms
Collins1984
.
Considering Feynman integrals as the generalized hypergeometric functions
Regge1967
,
one finds that the
D-
module of a Feynman diagram
Nasrollahpoursamami2016
is isomorphic to
Gel’fand-Kapranov-Zelevinsky (GKZ)
D-
module
Gelfand1987
;
Gelfand1988
;
Gelfand1988a
;
Gelfand1989
;
Gelfand1990
.
Correspondingly Feynman integrals satisfy indeed the systems of
holonomic linear partial differential equations (PDEs)
Kashiwara1976
whose singularities
are determined by the Landau singularities.

During the past decades, some progresses have been made on the subject.
Under the assumption of zero virtual masses, the Feynman integral of the 1-loop triangle
diagram is given as a linear combination of the fourth kind of Appell functions
Davydychev1
whose
arguments are the dimensionless ratios among the external momentum squared, and is simplified
further as a linear combination of the Gauss functions
{}_{2}F_{1}
through the quadratic
transformation
Davydychev1993NPB
in the literature
Davydychev2000
.
Taking some special assumptions on parameter space, the authors of Ref.
Davydychev3

obtain the multiple hypergeometric expressions of the scalar integral
C_{{}_{0}}
through
Mellin-Barnes representations. An algorithm to evaluate the scalar integrals of
1-loop vertex-type Feynman diagrams is presented in Ref.
Davydychev1992JPA
,
and a geometrical interpretation of the analytic expressions of the scalar integrals
of 1-loop
N-
point Feynman diagrams is also given in Ref.
Davydychev2006
.
Certainly the expressions of
C_{{}_{0}}
function can also be derived from expressions of the
1-loop massive
N-
point Feynman diagrams
Davydychev1991JMP
;
Davydychev1992JMP
.
In addition Feynman integrals of ladder diagrams with 3 or 4 external lines
can be evaluated through the parametric representation and Mellin-Barnes contour
integrals
Davydychev1993
.
Actually the 1-loop 2-point function
B_{{}_{0}}
can be expressed as a linear combination
of the Gauss functions
{}_{2}F_{1}
through the recurrence relations respecting time-space
dimension, the 1-loop 3-point function
C_{{}_{0}}
similarly is formulated as a linear
combination of the Appell functions
F_{{}_{1}}
, and the 1-loop 4-point function
D_{{}_{0}}

is formulated as a linear combination of the Lauricella functions

F_{{}_{s}}

Tarasov2000
;
Tarasov2003
, respectively.
The expression of
C_{{}_{0}}
is convenient
for analytic continuation and numerical evaluation because continuation of
F_{{}_{1}}

has been analyzed thoroughly. However, how to perform continuation
of the Lauricella function
F_{{}_{s}}
outside its convergent domain is still a challenge.
Expressing Feynman integrals as generalized hypergeometric functions in dimension regularization,
the authors of Ref.
Kalmykov2009
analyze the Laurent expansion of these hypergeometric
functions around
D=4
. The differential-reduction algorithm to evaluate those hypergeometric
functions can be found in Refs.
Bytev2010
;
Kalmykov2011
;
Bytev2013
;
Bytev2015
;
Bytev2016
.
A hypergeometric system of linear PDEs is given through the corresponding Mellin-Barnes
representation
Kalmykov2012
.
Some irreducible master integrals of sunset and bubble Feynman diagrams
are explicitly evaluated through Mellin-Barnes contour in Ref.
Kalmykov2017
.

GKZ-hypergeometric systems of Feynman integrals with codimension
=0,\;1

are presented in Refs.
Cruz2019
;
Klausen2019

through Lee-Pomeransky parametric representations
Lee2013
.
To construct canonical series solutions with suitable independent variables,
one should compute the restricted
D
-module of GKZ-hypergeometric system originating from
Lee-Pomeransky representations on corresponding hyperplane in the parameter
space
Oaku1997
;
Walther1999
;
Oaku2001
.
Certainly GKZ-hypergeometric systems of some Feynman diagrams
are obtained from Mellin-Barnes representations
Feng2018
;
Feng2019
;
Feng2020
through Miller’s
transformation
Miller68
;
Miller72
.
Ref.
Loebbert2020
explores the idea of bootstrap Feynman integrals using integrability,
Ref.
Klemm2020
applies the GKZ description of periods to solve the
l-loop banana amplitudes with their general mass dependence, and Ref.
Bonisch2021
completely clarifies
the analytic structure of all banana amplitudes with arbitrary masses, respectively.
The updated summary on some classical and modern aspects of hypergeometric
differential equations is given in literature
Reichelt2020
.
A mathematical package for integrating families of Feynman integrals
order-by order in the dimensional regulator from their GKZ-systems
is given in literature
Hidding2021
, and
a specialized integration algorithm for parametric Feynman integrals
with tame kinematics is also presented in literature
Borinsky2020
.
Actually Cohen-Macaulay Property of Feynman Integral indicates
that the process of finding a series representation of these
integrals is fully algorithmic
Tellander2021
, and regular
singularities of Feynman Integral are determined by its Landau Discriminant
Mizera2021
.
Ref.
Arkani-Hamed2022
introduces a class of polytopes to analyze
the structure of UV and IR divergences of general Feynman integrals in Schwinger parameter space.
Furthermore, Ref.
Chestnov2022
elaborates on the connection among GKZ-systems, de
Rham theory for twisted cohomology groups, and Pfaffian equations for Feynman Integrals.
Ref.
Ananthanarayan2022
presents a Mathematica package to find linear transformations
for some classes of multivariable hypergeometric functions. In literature
Berends1994

the class of
N
loop massive scalar self-energy diagrams with
N+1
propagators
is studied, and the new convergent series representations for the 2-loop sunset diagram
with 3 different propagator masses and external momentum are obtained in
Ref.
Ananthanarayan2019
. The relationship between Feynman diagrams and hypergeometric functions
is reviewed in the Ref.
Kalmykov2021
.

Since the fundamental solution systems of neighborhoods of different regular
singularities are different, and many equivalent fundamental solution systems
can be constructed corresponding to neighborhoods of same regular singularity.
When a Feynman integral is expressed as a linear combination of
hypergeometric functions of the fundamental solution system,
there are obvious differences among the equivalent representations accordingly.
Finding a framework for understanding these rambling results is undoubtedly
important on both theoretical and phenomenological aspects.
The fundamental solution systems of neighborhoods of different regular
singularities can be regarded as analytic continuations of each other.
Thus the analytic expression of Feynman integral can be obtained in the whole parameter space.

Nevertheless, one only obtains the fundamental solution systems in neighborhoods of the
regular singularities
0,\;\infty
using the GKZ-hypergeometric system of a general compact manifold.
Taking the Feynman integral as a linear combinations of hypergeometric functions from
the fundamental solution systems around different regular singularities and embedding
it in subvarieties of Grassmannians, one finds that the dual space of the GKZ-system
satisfied by the Feynman integral in the splitting coordinates determines the integer
lattice matrices, then writes the exponent matrices compatible with the integer
lattice matrices. A pair of integer lattice and exponent matrices uniquely determines
a generalized hypergeometric function where the geometric representation
Oxley2011

of its exponent matrix can clearly tell us whether or not the hypergeometric
function of a different pair of integer lattice and exponent matrices is
proportional to it in the nonempty intersection of their convergent regions.

Some hypergeometric functions are defined on the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{k,n}}

Gelfand1986-1

in a natural way, where the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{k,n}}
is the manifold of all

(k-1)
-dimensional projective subspaces of
(n-1)
-dimensional projective
space (
k<n
)
Schwartz1968
. In order to describe Grassmannian exactly,
one can understand the manifold
Z_{{}_{k,n}}
of
k\times n
matrices of rank
k

being a bundle space over the base space
G_{{}_{k,n}}
whose projection

Z_{{}_{k,n}}\rightarrow G_{{}_{k,n}}
assigns to each matrix
Z_{{}_{k,n}}

the
k
-dimensional projective subspace spanned by the row vectors of this matrix.
We consider an arbitrary smooth function on the manifold
Z_{{}_{k,n}}

satisfying the homogeneous condition

\displaystyle f(\chi_{{}_{1}}z_{{}_{1}},\cdots,\chi_{{}_{n}}z_{{}_{n}})=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\chi_{{}_{i}}^{\alpha_{{}_{i}}-1}f(z_{{}_{1}},\cdots,z_{{}_{n}})\;,\chi_{{}_{i}}\neq 0\;,

(2)

where
z_{{}_{i}}
,
(i=1,\cdots,n)
denotes the
i
-th column vector of the
k\times n

matrix
Z_{{}_{k,n}}
, and

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{{}_{i}}=n-k\;.

(3)

Let
t=(t_{{}_{1}},\cdots,t_{{}_{k}})
denote the local coordinates of the

k
-dimensional projective subspace, and the volume element

\displaystyle\omega(t)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}(-)^{i-1}dt_{{}_{1}}\wedge\cdots dt_{{}_{i-1}}\wedge dt_{{}_{i+1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dt_{{}_{k}}

(4)

thus the integral

\displaystyle F(z)=\int\limits_{S}f(t\cdot z)\omega(t)

(5)

satisfies the homogeneous conditions

\displaystyle F(g\cdot z)=|\det g|^{-1}F(z),\;\;g\in GL(k,C),

\displaystyle F(z\cdot\chi)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\chi_{{}_{i}}^{\alpha_{{}_{i}}-1}F(z),\;\;\chi={\rm diag}(\chi_{{}_{1}},\cdots,\chi_{{}_{n}})\;.

(6)

Here
S
is an arbitrary
(k-1)
-dimensional hypersurface enclosing the origin.
In this approach elements of the matrix
Z_{{}_{k\times n}}
are named as the splitting
local coordinates.
Obviously the first condition is equivalent to the system of partial differential
equations (PDEs)
Gelfand1980
;
Gelfand1986-2
;
Gelfand1990+a
;
Gelfand1992

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}z_{{}_{ij}}{\partial F\over\partial z_{{}_{i^{\prime}j}}}=-\delta_{{}_{i,i^{\prime}}}F,\;\;\;i\in[1,k],\;i^{\prime}\in[1,k],

(7)

with
\delta_{{}_{i,i^{\prime}}}
denoting the Kronecker symbol,
and the second condition of Eq.(
6
) is equivalent to the system of PDEs

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}z_{{}_{ij}}{\partial F\over\partial z_{{}_{ij}}}=(\alpha_{{}_{j}}-1)F,\;\;\;j\in[1,n],

(8)

respectively. Using the GKZ-system presented above, we can construct the fundamental
solution system which is composed of the hypergeometric functions of local splitting
coordinates
z_{{}_{ij}}

Gelfand1990+a
;
Gelfand1992
.

After embedding parametric representations of Feynman integrals in projective space,
we formulate the Feynman integrals as hypergeometric functions over Grassmannians.
Taking Feynman integrals of the 1-loop self-energy,
the massless 1-loop triangle, and the 2-loop vacuum diagrams as examples, we illuminate
how to construct canonical series solutions from those relevant GKZ-systems
Gelfand1990+a
;
Gelfand1992
,
whose convergent regions of those series can be derived by Horn’s study
Horn1889
.
Finally parametric representations of the 2-loop self energy diagrams with 3, 4 and 5 propagators
are presented respectively. Those Feynman integrals are embedded in
the subvarieties of Grassmannians by the parametric representations directly.
The adopted algorithm here is applied to obtain the fundamental solution systems of the GKZ-systems
associating with the subvarieties of the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{3,6}}
in Ref.
Gelfand1990+a
,
and all techniques in detail of this approach can be found in the long review article
Gelfand1992
.
In principle, we can embed the parametric representation of a given Feynman
integral into infinite subvarieties of Grassmannians.
However, the dimension of dual space of the GKZ-systems of most subvarieties
is much larger than the number of independent dimensionless ratios between the
square of external momentum and the square of virtual masses.
Correspondingly convergent regions of the obtained hypergeometric series solutions are empty.
In addition, we don’t state those mathematical concepts and theorems that have been used in our analyses here,
because they can be found in some well-known mathematical textbooks
M.Saito2000
;
M.E.Taylor12
;
L.J.Slater66
;
J.Leray1959
;
Cox1991
;
Cox1998
;
Sturmfels1995
;
Eisenbud1995
;
Coutinho1995
.

The generally strategy for analysing Feynman integral includes following steps
in this approach. First we embed parametric representation of Feynman integral in
the subvarieties of proper Grassmannians, and write out the GKZ-system satisfied by the
Feynman integral. Second the fundamental solution systems are constructed in
neighborhoods of regular singularities of the GKZ-system.
The integration constants, i.e. the combination coefficients, are determined
from the Feynman integral with some special kinematic parameters
Feng2018
;
Chetyrkin1980
;
Gu2020
.

Our presentation is organized as following. After embedding the Feynman integrals
in the subvarieties of Grassmannians appropriately, we derive the fundamental solution systems of Feynman
integrals of the 1-loop vacuum and 1-loop self energy in section
II
, respectively.
Then we present in detail how to embed parametric representation of the 1-loop massless triangle
diagrams in section
III
, and several equivalent fundamental solution systems
are presented in different neighborhoods of regular singularities.
In section
IV
, we embed Feynman integral of the 2-loop vacuum in the subvarieties
of Grassmannian
G_{{}_{4,8}}
, then present the hypergeometric series in neighborhoods
of regular singularities. Finally parametric representations of the 2-loop self energy diagrams
with 3, 4 and 5 propagators are presented respectively in section
V
,
and the conclusions are summarized in section
VI
.

II
The 1-loop self-energy

We first use the well-known 1-loop bubble diagram to illustrate how to embed
the corresponding Feynman integral into the subvarieties of Grassmannians, then construct its
fundamental solution system. The Feynman integral of the 1-loop bubble diagram is written as

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1VA}}(m^{2})=\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\int{d^{D}q\over(2\pi)^{D}}{1\over q^{2}-m^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 51.21504pt=-{i\Gamma(1-D/2)\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}{\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\over\Big(m^{2}\Big)^{1-D/2}}\;,

(9)

with
\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}
denoting the renormalization scale.
After performing the Wick rotation and integrating over angles,

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1VA}}(m^{2})=-{i\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\over(4\pi)^{D/2}\Gamma({D\over 2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}{dt_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{1}}^{D/2-1}\over t_{{}_{1}}+m^{2}}\;.

(10)

Introducing the homogeneous coordinate
t_{{}_{2}}=1
, one reformulates the scalar integral as

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1VA}}(m^{2})=-{i\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\over(4\pi)^{D/2}\Gamma({D\over 2})}\int{\omega(t)t_{{}_{1}}^{D/2-1}t_{{}_{2}}^{-D/2}\over t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}m^{2}}\;,

(11)

with the volume element of projective line
\omega(t)=t_{{}_{2}}dt_{{}_{1}}-t_{{}_{1}}dt_{{}_{2}}
.
Eq.(
11
) can be taken as an integral on the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{2,3}}

with splitting local coordinates as

\displaystyle A^{1V}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;1\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;m^{2}\end{array}\right)\;,

whose geometric representation is 3 non-overlapping points on a projective line.
In the matrix
A^{1V}
, the first row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{1}}
,
the second row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{2}}
, the first column represents
the power function
t_{{}_{1}}^{D/2-1}
, the second column represents the power function
t_{{}_{2}}^{-D/2}
,
and the third column represents the power function
(z_{{}_{1,3}}t_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2,3}}t_{{}_{2}})^{-1}=(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}m^{2})^{-1}
, respectively.
In the above splitting coordinates, the Feynman integral of the 1-loop bubble
satisfies the following GKZ-system

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}+\vartheta_{{}_{1,3}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1VA}}=-A_{{}_{1VA}}\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,3}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1VA}}=-A_{{}_{1VA}}\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}A_{{}_{1VA}}=({D\over 2}-1)A_{{}_{1VA}}\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}A_{{}_{1VA}}=-{D\over 2}A_{{}_{1VA}}\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,3}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1VA}}=-A_{{}_{1VA}}\;,

(15)

where the Euler operator
\vartheta_{{}_{i,j}}=z_{{}_{i,j}}\partial/\partial z_{{}_{i,j}}
.
The dual space of the above GKZ-system is the null space
\{0\}
. Thus
the exponent matrix is written uniquely as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\;\;{D\over 2}-1&\;\;0&\;\;-{D\over 2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;-{D\over 2}&\;\;{D\over 2}-1\end{array}\right)\;,

and the matrix of integer lattice is the zero matrix
0_{{}_{2\times 3}}
.
Using the above matrices of exponent and integer lattice, one obtains the unique solution of
the GKZ-system satisfied by
A_{{}_{1VA}}(m^{2})
as

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1VA}}(m^{2})={C_{{}_{1VA}}^{(12)}\over\Gamma(1-{D\over 2})\Gamma({D\over 2})}\Big(m^{2}\Big)^{D/2-1}\propto{1^{-D/2}\over\Gamma(1-{D\over 2})\Gamma({D\over 2})}\Big(m^{2}\Big)^{D/2-1}\;,

(19)

with the integration constant

\displaystyle C_{{}_{1VA}}^{(12)}=-{i\Gamma^{2}(1-{D\over 2})\Gamma({D\over 2})\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\;.

(20)

The above solution is obtained where we take the 1st and 2nd column vectors
as the basis of the projective line. Because

\displaystyle\det A_{{}_{13}}^{1V}=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\;\;1&\;\;1\\
\;\;0&\;\;m^{2}\end{array}\right)=m^{2}\neq 0\;,

\displaystyle\det A_{{}_{23}}^{1V}=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\;\;0&\;\;1\\
\;\;1&\;\;m^{2}\end{array}\right)=-1\neq 0\;,

we can also choose
\{(1,\;0)^{T},\;(1,\;m^{2})^{T}\}
or
\{(0,\;1)^{T},\;(1,\;m^{2})^{T}\}
as the basis of
the projective line respectively. As we choose the vectors
\{(1,\;0)^{T},\;(1,\;m^{2})^{T}\}

as the basis of the projective line, the splitting local coordinate matrix is written as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\;\;1&\;\;-{1\over m^{2}}&\;\;0\\
\;\;0&\;\;{1\over m^{2}}&\;\;1\end{array}\right)\;.

Using the unique matrix of exponents

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\;\;{D\over 2}-1&\;\;-{D\over 2}&\;\;0\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;-1\end{array}\right)\;,

and the integer lattice matrix
0_{{}_{2\times 3}}
, one obtains the single solution as

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1VA}}(m^{2})={C_{{}_{1VA}}^{(13)}\over\det(A_{{}_{13}}^{1V})\Gamma(1-{D\over 2})\Gamma(1)}\Big(-{1\over m^{2}}\Big)^{0}\Big({1\over m^{2}}\Big)^{-D/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 51.21504pt={C_{{}_{1VA}}^{(13)}\over\Gamma(1-{D\over 2})}\Big(m^{2}\Big)^{D/2-1}\;.

(33)

Matching with Eq.(
9
), we get the integration constant as

\displaystyle C_{{}_{1VA}}^{(13)}=-{i\Gamma^{2}(1-{D\over 2})\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\;.

(34)

Similarly taking the vectors
\{(0,\;1)^{T},\;(1,\;m^{2})^{T}\}

as the basis of the projective line, we write the splitting local coordinate matrix as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\;\;-m^{2}&\;\;1&\;\;0\\
\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;1\end{array}\right)\;.

Using the matrix of exponents in Eq.(
II
) and the
integer lattice
0_{{}_{2\times 3}}
, we write the solution as

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1VA}}(m^{2})={C_{{}_{1VA}}^{(23)}\over\det(A_{{}_{23}}^{1V})\Gamma({D\over 2})\Gamma(1)}\Big(-m^{2}\Big)^{D/2-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 51.21504pt=-{(-)^{D/2-1}C_{{}_{1VA}}^{(23)}\over\Gamma({D\over 2})}\Big(m^{2}\Big)^{D/2-1}\;.

(38)

From Eq.(
9
), the integration constant is given as

\displaystyle C_{{}_{1VA}}^{(23)}={i(-1)^{1-D/2}\Gamma(1-{D\over 2})\Gamma({D\over 2})\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\;.

(39)

Note that on the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{2,3}}
, there is only one choice of
the exponent matrix compatible with the integer lattice
0_{{}_{2\times 3}}
. This is the reason why
three different choices on the basis of the projective line induce the same solution
of the GKZ-system which is proportional to
(m^{2})^{D/2-1}
.

It should be emphasized that the matrix of splitting local coordinates in Eq.(
II
)
is not uniquely determined. In fact, the columns of the matrix represent 3 distinct points on the
projective line. Multiplying each column by a non-zero constant does not change the
corresponding geometric representation of the matrix Eq.(
II
). Correspondingly those
fundamental solutions are completely consistent with each other up to a constant scalar multiple.

In the following we apply this method to present the subvarieties of
Grassmannians embedded by the Feynman integral of the 1-loop self-energy
diagram, then construct the fundamental solution systems which are
composed of linear independent hypergeometric functions in
neighborhoods of the regular singularities. As mentioned above,
the analytic expression of the Feynman integral is expressed as the linear combinations
of these hypergeometric functions of the fundamental solution systems
in different coordinate neighborhoods of regular singularities, whose
combination coefficients are determined by the Feynman integral on an ordinary
point or some regular singularities.
In the dimension regularization, the Feynman integral of the 1-loop self-energy
diagram is written as

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\int{d^{D}q\over(2\pi)^{D}}{1\over(q^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})((q+p)^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2})}\;.

(40)

Adopting Feynman parametric representation, we get the integral of zero virtual
masses as

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,0)={i(-1)^{D/2-2}\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 82.51282pt\times\int_{0}^{1}dt_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{1}}^{D/2-2}(p^{2}t_{{}_{1}}-p^{2})^{D/2-2}

\displaystyle\hskip 68.28644pt={i(-1)^{D/2-2}\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 82.51282pt\times\int_{0}^{1}\omega(t)t_{{}_{1}}^{D/2-2}t_{{}_{2}}^{2-D}(p^{2}t_{{}_{1}}+p^{2}t_{{}_{2}})^{D/2-2}\;.

(41)

In the last equation
t_{{}_{2}}=-1
, and
\omega(t)

is the volume element of the projective line.
Eq.(
41
) can be embedded in the subvariety of the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{2,3}}

with splitting local coordinates as

\displaystyle A^{1SE}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;p^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;p^{2}\end{array}\right)\;.

In the matrix above, the first row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{1}}
,
the second row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{2}}
, the first column represents
the power function
t_{{}_{1}}^{D/2-2}
, the second column represents the power function
t_{{}_{2}}^{2-D}
,
and the third column represents the power function
(z_{{}_{1,3}}t_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2,3}}t_{{}_{2}})^{D/2-2}=(t_{{}_{1}}p^{2}+t_{{}_{2}}p^{2})^{D/2-2}
, respectively.
The Feynman integral satisfies the following GKZ-system

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}+\vartheta_{{}_{1,3}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}=-A_{{}_{1SE}}\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,3}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}=-A_{{}_{1SE}}\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}A_{{}_{1SE}}=({D\over 2}-2)A_{{}_{1SE}}\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}A_{{}_{1SE}}=(2-D)A_{{}_{1SE}}\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,3}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}=({D\over 2}-2)A_{{}_{1SE}}\;.

(45)

Obviously the dual space of the GKZ-system of Eq.(
45
) is the null
space
\{0\}
which is consistent with the exponent matrix

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\;\;{D\over 2}-2&\;\;0&\;\;1-{D\over 2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;2-D&\;\;D-3\end{array}\right)\;.

Using the matrix of exponents, one obtains the solution of
the GKZ-system of Eq.(
45
)

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,0)={C_{{}_{1SE}}^{(0)}\over\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})\Gamma(D-2)}\Big(p^{2}\Big)^{1-D/2}\Big(p^{2}\Big)^{D-3}

\displaystyle\hskip 68.28644pt={C_{{}_{1SE}}^{(0)}\over\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})\Gamma(D-2)}\Big(p^{2}\Big)^{D/2-2}\;,

(49)

with the integration constant

\displaystyle C_{{}_{1SE}}^{(0)}={i\Gamma^{2}(2-{D\over 2})\Gamma^{2}(1-{D\over 2})\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}\;.

(50)

The solution Eq.(
49
) is obtained where we take the first and second column vectors of Eq.(
II
)
as the basis of the projective line. Because

\displaystyle\det A_{{}_{13}}^{1SE}=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\;\;1&\;\;p^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;p^{2}\end{array}\right)=p^{2}\neq 0\;,

\displaystyle\det A_{{}_{23}}^{1SE}=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\;\;0&\;\;p^{2}\\
\;\;1&\;\;p^{2}\end{array}\right)=-p^{2}\neq 0\;,

we can choose
\{(1,\;0)^{T},\;(p^{2},\;p^{2})^{T}\}
or
\{(0,\;1)^{T},\;(p^{2},\;p^{2})^{T}\}
as the basis of
the projective line respectively. Using two different choices of the basis of the projective line,
the solutions of the GKZ-system are all proportional to
(p^{2})^{D/2-2}
.

Along with increasing of the number of independent arguments of the Feynman integral,
the dimension of dual space of the GKZ-system increases. The bases of the dual space
determine the PDEs satisfied by the Feynman integral in the combined local coordinates.

II.1
The fundamental solution systems as
m_{{}_{1}}^{2}=0

As
m_{{}_{1}}^{2}=0
,
p^{2}\neq 0
, and
m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\neq 0
,
Feynman parametric representation of the 1-loop self energy is given as

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})={i\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\over(-)^{2-D/2}(4\pi)^{D/2}}\int_{0}^{1}dt_{{}_{1}}dt_{{}_{3}}{t_{{}_{1}}^{D/2-2}\delta(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{3}}-1)\over(t_{{}_{3}}p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{2-D/2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt={i\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\over(-)^{1-D/2}(4\pi)^{D/2}}\int\omega_{{}_{3}}(t){t_{{}_{1}}^{D/2-2}t_{{}_{2}}^{2-D}\delta(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}+t_{{}_{3}})\over(t_{{}_{3}}p^{2}+t_{{}_{2}}m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{2-D/2}}\;,

(57)

where

\displaystyle\omega_{{}_{k}}(t)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}(-)^{i-1}t_{{}_{i}}dt_{{}_{1}}\wedge\cdots\widehat{dt_{{}_{i}}}\wedge\cdots dt_{{}_{k}}

(58)

denotes the volume element of
(k-1)
-dimension projective space, and the
hat symbol implies that the wedge product does not involve
dt_{{}_{i}}
.
Eq.(
57
) can be taken as an integral on the subvariety of the
Grassmannian
G_{{}_{3,5}}
with splitting local coordinates as

\displaystyle A^{(1)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;0\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;1&\;\;p^{2}\end{array}\right)\;.

Here the first row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{1}}
,
the second row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{2}}
,
and the third row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{3}}
, respectively.
Meanwhile the first column represents the power function
t_{{}_{1}}^{D/2-2}
,
the second column represents the power function
t_{{}_{2}}^{2-D}
, the third column
represents the power function
t_{{}_{3}}^{0}=1
,
the fourth column represents the function
\delta(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}+t_{{}_{3}})
,
and the fifth column represents
(z_{{}_{1,5}}t_{{}_{1}}+z_{{}_{2,5}}t_{{}_{2}}+z_{{}_{3,5}}t_{{}_{3}})^{D/2-2}=(t_{{}_{2}}m_{{}_{2}}^{2}+t_{{}_{3}}p^{2})^{D/2-2}
, respectively.
The geometric description of the matrix of Eq.(
II.1
) can be drawn in
2-dimension projective plane
Oxley2011
as Fig.
1
.

Figure 1:
The geometric description of the matrix in
Eq.(
II.1
) on the projective plane
CP^{2}
, where the points
1,\cdots,5

denote the indices of columns of the
3\times 5
matrix.

Accordingly the Feynman integral satisfies the following GKZ-system in splitting local coordinates

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}+\vartheta_{{}_{1,4}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=-A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,5}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=-A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{3,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,5}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=-A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=({D\over 2}-2)A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=(2-D)A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{3,3}}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=0\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,4}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=-A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{2,5}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,5}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=({D\over 2}-2)A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

(63)

which induces the exponent matrix as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\;\;{D\over 2}-2&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1-{D\over 2}&\;\;0\\
\;\;0&\;\;2-D&\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}&\;\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{3,4}}&\;\alpha_{{}_{3,5}}\end{array}\right)\;.

Where the exponents
\alpha_{{}_{i,j}}
satisfy the following relations

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}=D-3,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,4}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,5}}=-1,

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,4}}={D\over 2}-2,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,5}}={D\over 2}-2.

(68)

Furthermore, the dual space of the GKZ-system of Eq.(
63
) is spanned by
a
3\times 5
matrix
(0_{{}_{3\times 3}}\Big|E_{{}_{3}}^{(1)})
with

\displaystyle E_{{}_{3}}^{(1)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\;\;0&\;\;0\\
\;\;1&\;\;-1\\
\;\;-1&\;\;1\end{array}\right)\;.

The base of the dual space implies the Feynman integral also satisfying the PDE as

\displaystyle{\partial^{2}A_{{}_{1SE}}\over\partial z_{{}_{2,4}}\partial z_{{}_{3,5}}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})={\partial^{2}A_{{}_{1SE}}\over\partial z_{{}_{2,5}}\partial z_{{}_{3,4}}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;.

(73)

When the exponent matrix is given, the Feynman integral can be formally expressed as

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},0,m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=\prod\limits_{i,j}z_{{}_{i,j}}^{\alpha_{{}_{i,j}}}\varphi({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over p^{2}})\;,

(74)

where
\varphi(x)
satisfies the PDE in the combined coordinate
x
as

\displaystyle x\Big[\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}\alpha_{{}_{3,5}}+(1-\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}-\alpha_{{}_{3,5}})x{d\over dx}-x^{2}{d^{2}\over dx^{2}}\Big]\varphi(x)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt=\Big[\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}\alpha_{{}_{3,4}}+(1+\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,4}})x{d\over dx}-x^{2}{d^{2}\over dx^{2}}\Big]\varphi(x)\;.

(75)

As
\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}\alpha_{{}_{3,4}}=0
this equation recovers the well-known Gauss equation.
Note that the combined local coordinate
x=m_{{}_{2}}^{2}/p^{2}=z_{{}_{2,5}}z_{{}_{3,4}}/z_{{}_{2,4}}z_{{}_{3,5}}

is determined by the base of the dual space.

(0_{{}_{3\times 3}}\Big|nE_{{}_{3}}^{(1)})
(

of the exponents. The first choice is written as

Actually the last expression can be taken as the analytical continuation

\widehat{(14)}
,
or obtained from that of Eq.(

) through the permutation
\widehat{(23)}
, respectively.
Because the geometric description of Eq.(

) is same as that of Eq.(

),
Eq.(

), and Eq.(

), the functions

\psi_{{}_{\{1,2,5\}}}^{(4)}\sim\psi_{{}_{\{1,3,5\}}}^{(4)}\sim\psi_{{}_{\{2,4,5\}}}^{(2)}\sim\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,5\}}}^{(2)}

in nonempty intersection of their convergent regions.
Including appropriate scalar factors, each hypergeometric function is analytic continuation
of other functions in the union of their convergent regions.

The matrix of integer lattice
-nE_{{}_{3}}^{(2)}
permits two choices of the exponents.
The first choice is

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}=-1,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}=0,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}={D\over 2}-1,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,2}}=2-D\;,

(170)

whose geometric representation is presented in Fig.

(2)
with

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}={D\over 2}-2,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}=1-{D\over 2},\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}=0,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,2}}=1-{D\over 2}\;.

(171)

whose geometric representation is presented in Fig.

,

\{(b,c),\;(d,e)\}=\{(1,4),\;(2,5)\}
, respectively.
Adopting the matrix of integer lattice
-nE_{{}_{3}}^{(2)}

and the matrices of exponents, we obtain a fundamental solution
system composed of two hypergeometric solutions as

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,5\}}}^{(3)}(p^{2},\;0,\;m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D/2-1}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}{\Gamma(D-2+n)\over\Gamma({D\over 2}+n)}\Big({p^{2}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big)^{n}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,5\}}}^{(4)}(p^{2},\;0,\;m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over p^{2}}\Big)^{D/2-1}(p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D/2-2}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}{\Gamma({D\over 2}-1+n)\over n!}\Big({p^{2}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big)^{n}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt=(p^{2})^{1-D/2}(p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D-3}\;,

(172)

which are convergent in the region
|p^{2}/(p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2})|\leq 1
.
Since the basis
\{3,4,5\}
of the projective
plane composed of the third, fourth and fifth columns of the matrix Eq.(

)
is obtained from the basis
\{1,2,3\}
through the permutation
\widehat{(14)(25)}
,

through the permutation
\widehat{(14)}
,
or equivalently obtained from the basis

through the permutation
\widehat{(25)}
, respectively.
Accordingly the geometric description of Eq.(

)
is taken from that of Eq.(

\widehat{(14)(25)}
,
or taken from that of Eq.(

) through the permutation
\widehat{(14)}
,
or taken from that of Eq.(

) through the permutation
\widehat{(25)}
, respectively.
Because the geometric description of Eq.(

) is same as that of Eq.(

),

), and Eq.(

), the functions

\psi_{{}_{\{1,2,3\}}}^{(2)}\sim\psi_{{}_{\{1,3,5\}}}^{(1)}\sim\psi_{{}_{\{2,3,4\}}}^{(2)}\sim\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,5\}}}^{(3)}

in nonempty intersection of their convergent regions.
Similarly the geometric description of Eq.(

)

\widehat{(14)(25)}
,

in nonempty intersection of their convergent regions.

II.2
The fundamental solution system in general case

As the 1-loop self energy diagram involves 2 nonzero virtual masses, the integer
lattice is spanned by two linear independent vectors, and the number of
exponential matrices compatible with them also increases accordingly.

Using Feynman parametric representation, we write the 1-loop self energy in the general case as

\displaystyle A^{1S}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\int{d^{D}q\over(2\pi)^{D}}{1\over(q^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})((q+p)^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2})}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt={i\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})\over(-)^{2-D/2}(4\pi)^{D/2}}\int_{0}^{1}dt_{{}_{1}}dt_{{}_{2}}\delta(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}-1)

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\times(t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{2}}p^{2}-t_{{}_{1}}m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-t_{{}_{2}}m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D/2-2}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=-{i\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{2-D/2}\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})\over(-)^{2-D/2}(4\pi)^{D/2}}\int\omega_{{}_{3}}(t)\delta(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}+t_{{}_{3}})

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\times t_{{}_{3}}^{2-D}(t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{2}}p^{2}+t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{3}}m_{{}_{1}}^{2}+t_{{}_{2}}t_{{}_{3}}m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D/2-2}\;.

(173)

The integral can be embedded in the subvariety of the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{3,6}}

where the first row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{1}}
,
the second row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{2}}
,
and the third row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{3}}
, respectively.
Meanwhile the first column represents the power function
t_{{}_{1}}^{0}=1
,
the second column represents the power function
t_{{}_{2}}^{0}=1
, the third column
represents the power function
t_{{}_{3}}^{2-D}
,
the fourth column represents the function
\delta(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}+t_{{}_{3}})
. In order to
embed the homogeneous polynomial
(t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{2}}p^{2}+t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{3}}m_{{}_{1}}^{2}+t_{{}_{2}}t_{{}_{3}}m_{{}_{2}}^{2})

as the fifth and sixth columns of the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{3,6}}
, we rewrite

\displaystyle t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{2}}p^{2}+t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{3}}m_{{}_{1}}^{2}+t_{{}_{2}}t_{{}_{3}}m_{{}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt=z_{{}_{1,\widehat{\sigma}_{{}_{1}}(5)}}z_{{}_{2,\widehat{\sigma}_{{}_{1}}(6)}}t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{2}}+z_{{}_{1,\widehat{\sigma}_{{}_{2}}(5)}}z_{{}_{3,\widehat{\sigma}_{{}_{2}}(6)}}t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{3}}+z_{{}_{2,\widehat{\sigma}_{{}_{3}}(5)}}z_{{}_{3,\widehat{\sigma}_{{}_{3}}(6)}}t_{{}_{2}}t_{{}_{3}}\;,

(174)

where
\widehat{\sigma}_{{}_{i}},\;(i=1,\;2,\;3)
are elements of the permutation
group
S_{{}_{2}}=\{\widehat{e},\;\widehat{(56)}\}
on the column indices
5,\;6
.
Taking
\widehat{\sigma}_{{}_{1}}=\widehat{\sigma}_{{}_{3}}=\widehat{(56)}
,
\widehat{\sigma}_{{}_{2}}=\widehat{e}
,
we have

\displaystyle z_{{}_{1,6}}z_{{}_{2,5}}=p^{2}\;,\;\;z_{{}_{1,5}}z_{{}_{3,6}}=m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\;,\;\;z_{{}_{2,6}}z_{{}_{3,5}}=m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\;.

(175)

The solution of Eq.(
175
)

\displaystyle z_{{}_{1,5}}=z_{{}_{2,5}}=z_{{}_{3,5}}=1\;,\;\;z_{{}_{1,6}}=p^{2}\;,\;\;z_{{}_{3,6}}=m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\;,\;\;z_{{}_{2,6}}=m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\;,

(176)

indicates that the splitting local coordinates are written as

\displaystyle A^{(1S)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\;1&\;0&\;0&\;1&\;1&\;p^{2}\\
\;0&\;1&\;0&\;1&\;1&\;m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\\
\;0&\;0&\;1&\;1&\;1&\;m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\end{array}\right)\;,

whose geometric description is drawn in Fig.
3
.

Figure 3:
The geometric description of the matrix in
Eq.(
II.2
) on the projective plane
CP^{2}
, where the points
1,\cdots,6

denote the indices of columns of the
3\times 6
matrix, and
4,\;5
denote
a double point.

Taking Feynman parametric representation of the 1-loop self energy
as a function of the splitting local coordinates,

\displaystyle A^{1S}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=\Psi^{1S}(z_{{}_{1}},\;\cdots,\;z_{{}_{6}})\;,

(181)

we obviously have

\displaystyle\Psi^{1S}(\chi_{{}_{1}}z_{{}_{1}},\;\cdots,\;\chi_{{}_{6}}z_{{}_{6}})=\chi_{{}_{3}}^{2-D}\chi_{{}_{4}}^{-1}\chi_{{}_{5}}^{D/2-2}\chi_{{}_{6}}^{D/2-2}\Psi^{1S}(z_{{}_{1}},\;\cdots,\;z_{{}_{6}})\;.

(182)

Where
z_{{}_{i}}\;(i=1,\cdots,6)
denotes the i-th column vector in the matrix Eq.(
II.2
),
and
\chi_{{}_{i}}
is a nonzero constant, respectively.
If we choose the first, second and third column vectors of
A^{(1S)}
as the basis of the projective plane,
constructed hypergeometric functions contain redundant dependent variables.
However
\det(A_{{}_{\{1,2,4\}}}^{(1S)})=1
where the matrix
A_{{}_{\{1,2,4\}}}^{(1S)}
denotes
the submatrix of
A^{(1S)}
composed of the first, second, and fourth columns, one derives

\displaystyle Z_{{}_{3\times 6}}=\Big(A_{{}_{\{1,2,4\}}}^{(1S)}\Big)^{-1}\cdot A^{(1S)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;-1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;-1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;1&\;\;1&\;\;m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\end{array}\right)\;.

Accordingly, the Feynman integral satisfies the following GKZ-system in splitting local coordinates

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}+\vartheta_{{}_{1,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{1,6}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=-A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,6}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=-A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{3,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,5}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,6}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=-A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=0\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=0\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,3}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=(2-D)A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{3,4}}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=-A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{3,5}}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=({D\over 2}-2)A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,6}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,6}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,6}}\Big\}A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=({D\over 2}-2)A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;.

(187)

Obviously the matrix of exponents is written as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{1,3}}&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{2,3}}&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{3,3}}&\;\;-1&\;\;{D\over 2}-2&\;\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}\end{array}\right)\;,

where the matrix elements satisfy the relations

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,3}}+\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}=-1\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,3}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}=-1\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,3}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}=2-{D\over 2}\;,

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,3}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,3}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,3}}=2-D\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}={D\over 2}-2\;.

(192)

Correspondingly there are 12 choices on the matrix of integer lattice whose
submatrix composed of the third and
sixth columns is formulated as
\pm n_{{}_{1}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(i)}\pm n_{{}_{2}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(j)}
,
where
n_{{}_{1,2}}\geq 0
,
(i,\;j)\in\{(1,\;2),\;(1,\;3),\;(2,\;3)\}
,
and other elements are all zero. Furthermore, the matrix

\displaystyle E_{{}_{3}}^{(1)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\;0&\;0\\
\;1&\;-1\\
\;-1&\;1\end{array}\right)\;,

and the matrices
E_{{}_{3}}^{(2)},\;E_{{}_{3}}^{(3)}
are presented in Eq.(
II.1
)
and Eq.(
II.1
), respectively.
Note that
E_{{}_{3}}^{(3)}=E_{{}_{3}}^{(2)}-E_{{}_{3}}^{(1)}
, two linear independent bases of
integer lattice matrices
E_{{}_{3}}^{(1)}
,
E_{{}_{3}}^{(2)}
induce two independent PDEs as:

\displaystyle{\partial^{2}A^{(1S)}\over\partial z_{{}_{1,3}}\partial z_{{}_{3,6}}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})={\partial^{2}A^{(1S)}\over\partial z_{{}_{1,6}}\partial z_{{}_{3,3}}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;,

\displaystyle{\partial^{2}A^{(1S)}\over\partial z_{{}_{2,3}}\partial z_{{}_{3,6}}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})={\partial^{2}A^{(1S)}\over\partial z_{{}_{2,6}}\partial z_{{}_{3,3}}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\;.

(197)

When the exponential matrix is given, the Feynman integral can be formally expressed as

\displaystyle A_{{}_{1SE}}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})=\prod\limits_{i,j}z_{{}_{i,j}}^{\alpha_{{}_{i,j}}}\varphi(1-{p^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}},1-{m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}})\;,

(198)

where
(i,j)\in\{(1,3),(1,6),(2,3),(2,6),(3,3),(3,6)\}
, and
\varphi(x_{{}_{1}},x_{{}_{2}})

satisfies the PDEs in the combined local coordinates
x_{{}_{1}}=z_{{}_{1,6}}z_{{}_{3,3}}/(z_{{}_{1,3}}z_{{}_{3,6}})

=1-p^{2}/m_{{}_{1}}^{2}
,
x_{{}_{2}}=z_{{}_{2,6}}z_{{}_{3,3}}/(z_{{}_{2,3}}z_{{}_{3,6}})
=1-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}/m_{{}_{1}}^{2}
as

\displaystyle x_{{}_{1}}\Big[\alpha_{{}_{1,3}}\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}+(1-\alpha_{{}_{1,3}}-\alpha_{{}_{3,6}})x_{{}_{1}}{\partial\over\partial x_{{}_{1}}}-\alpha_{{}_{1,3}}x_{{}_{2}}{\partial\over\partial x_{{}_{2}}}+x_{{}_{1}}x_{{}_{2}}{\partial^{2}\over\partial x_{{}_{1}}\partial x_{{}_{2}}}+x_{{}_{1}}^{2}{\partial^{2}\over\partial x_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big]\varphi(x_{{}_{1}},x_{{}_{2}})

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt=\Big[\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}\alpha_{{}_{3,3}}+(1+\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,3}})x_{{}_{1}}{\partial\over\partial x_{{}_{1}}}+\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}x_{{}_{2}}{\partial\over\partial x_{{}_{2}}}+x_{{}_{1}}x_{{}_{2}}{\partial^{2}\over\partial x_{{}_{1}}\partial x_{{}_{2}}}+x_{{}_{1}}^{2}{\partial^{2}\over\partial x_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big]\varphi(x_{{}_{1}},x_{{}_{2}})\;,

\displaystyle x_{{}_{2}}\Big[\alpha_{{}_{2,3}}\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}-\alpha_{{}_{2,3}}x_{{}_{1}}{\partial\over\partial x_{{}_{1}}}+(1-\alpha_{{}_{2,3}}-\alpha_{{}_{3,6}})x_{{}_{2}}{\partial\over\partial x_{{}_{2}}}+x_{{}_{1}}x_{{}_{2}}{\partial^{2}\over\partial x_{{}_{1}}\partial x_{{}_{2}}}+x_{{}_{2}}^{2}{\partial^{2}\over\partial x_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big]\varphi(x_{{}_{1}},x_{{}_{2}})

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt=\Big[\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}\alpha_{{}_{3,3}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}x_{{}_{1}}{\partial\over\partial x_{{}_{1}}}+(1+\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,3}})x_{{}_{2}}{\partial\over\partial x_{{}_{2}}}+x_{{}_{1}}x_{{}_{2}}{\partial^{2}\over\partial x_{{}_{1}}\partial x_{{}_{2}}}+x_{{}_{2}}^{2}{\partial^{2}\over\partial x_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big]\varphi(x_{{}_{1}},x_{{}_{2}})\;.

(199)

As
\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}=\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}=0
, the PDEs are changed into
the first type Appell PDEs. The holonomic rank of the ideal composed of the
PDEs is 3, which indicates the fundamental
solution system constituted by three linear independent hypergeometric
functions in neighborhoods of regular singularities.
It is known that the fourth type Appell function cannot be embedded into
Grassmannians
Gelfand1989
. Nevertheless, including the appropriate power factor
and the transformation of the variables, one proves that the Feynman integral presented by
the fourth Appell functions
Feng2018
;
Feng2020
satisfies the above PDEs.
Those matrices of integer lattice permit 12 possibilities of the exponents.
Corresponding to the integer lattice
n_{{}_{1}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(3)}+n_{{}_{2}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(2)}

the choice of exponents is written as

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,3}}=1-{D\over 2}\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}={D\over 2}-2\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,3}}=-1\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,3}}=2-{D\over 2},\;\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}=0\;,

(200)

whose geometric representation is presented in Fig.

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,3}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}=-1\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,3}}=-{D\over 2}\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}={D\over 2}-1\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,3}}=2-{D\over 2},\;\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}=0\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{1,2,4\}}}^{(1)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim(m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2})^{D/2-2}\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{\Gamma({D\over 2}-2+n_{{}_{2}})\over n_{{}_{2}}!}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Big({m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 96.73918pt\sim{(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(p^{2})^{2-D/2}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{1,2,4\}}}^{(2)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D/2-1}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{\Gamma({D\over 2}-2+n_{{}_{2}})\over n_{{}_{2}}!}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Big({m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 96.73918pt\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{2-D/2}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{1,2,4\}}}^{(3)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D/2}\over(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})}\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{\Gamma(D-2+n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}})\over\Gamma({D\over 2}+1+n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Big({m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}\;,

(203)

whose intersection of their convergent regions is nonempty proper subset
of the whole parameter space. Three hypergeometric functions constitute a

Correspondingly the linear independent hypergeometric functions are

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{1,2,4\}}}^{(4)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{2-D/2}(m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2})^{D-3}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{\Gamma({D\over 2}-2+n_{{}_{2}})\over n_{{}_{2}}!}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Big({m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}\\

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,2}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,2}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}={D\over 2}-2\;.

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{1,2,4\}}}^{(8)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{2-D/2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{(-)^{n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}}}\Gamma({D\over 2}-2+n_{{}_{2}})\over n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma(1-n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times{1\over\Gamma(D-2+n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}})}\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{2-D/2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{1,3,4\}}}^{(9)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D/2-1}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{\Gamma(D-3+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})\Gamma(3-D+n_{{}_{2}})\over n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma({D\over 2}+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D/2-1}(m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2})^{D-3}\over(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2})^{D-2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}}=0}^{\infty}{\Gamma(D-3+n_{{}_{1}})\over\Gamma({D\over 2}+n_{{}_{1}})}\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\;,

(242)

Because
\det(A_{{}_{\{2,3,4\}}}^{(1S)})=1
, we choose the second, third, fourth
column vectors composing of the basis of the projective plane, and find

\displaystyle\Big(A_{{}_{\{2,3,4\}}}^{(1S)}\Big)^{-1}\cdot A^{(1S)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\;\;-1&\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}\\
\;\;-1&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2}\\
\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;1&\;\;p^{2}\end{array}\right)\;.

Obviously the matrix of exponents is written as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\;\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}\\
\;\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}&\;\;0&\;\;2-D&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}\\
\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;-1&\;\;{D\over 2}-2&\;\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}\end{array}\right)\;,

where the matrix elements satisfy the relations

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}+\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}=-1\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}=D-3\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}=2-{D\over 2}\;,

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,6}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,6}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,6}}={D\over 2}-2\;.

\displaystyle\Big(A_{{}_{\{2,4,6\}}}^{(1S)}\Big)^{-1}\cdot A^{(1S)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\;\;-{m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2}}&\;\;1&\;\;-{m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}\over m_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p^{2}}&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0\\

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,3}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,3}}+\alpha_{{}_{3,3}}=2-D\;.

-n_{{}_{1}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(3)}-n_{{}_{2}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}=D-3\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}=3-D\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,2}}={D\over 2}-1\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}=0,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,2}}=1-{D\over 2}\;,

(304)

whose geometric representation is presented in Fig.
4
(3)
with
(a,b)=

(4,5)
,
\{(c,d)
,
(e,f)\}=

\{(1,3)
,
(2,6)\}
.
The exponents of the integer lattice

-n_{{}_{1}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(2)}-n_{{}_{2}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(1)}
are

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}=D-3\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}=2-{D\over 2}\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,2}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}=1-{D\over 2},\;\alpha_{{}_{3,2}}=0\;,

(305)

whose geometric representation is drawn in Fig.
4
(1)
with
\{(a,b)
,
(c,d)\}=

\{(2,6)
,
(4,5)\}
,
\{e,f\}=

\{1,3\}
.
Accordingly the hypergeometric functions are written as

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(4)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(p^{2})^{2-D/2}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{(-)^{n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}}}\Gamma({D\over 2}-2+n_{{}_{1}})\over n_{{}_{1}}!n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma(1-n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times{\Gamma({D\over 2}-1+n_{{}_{2}})\over\Gamma(D-2+n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}})}\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})\over p^{2}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\sim{(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(p^{2})^{2-D/2}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(5)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(p^{2})^{D/2-1}\over(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D-3}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{\Gamma(1-{D\over 2}+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})\Gamma(3-D+n_{{}_{1}})\over n_{{}_{1}}!n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma(4-D+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Gamma({D\over 2}-1+n_{{}_{2}})\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})\over p^{2}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over p^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(6)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{2-D/2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{(-)^{n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}}}\Gamma(3-D+n_{{}_{1}})\Gamma({D\over 2}-2+n_{{}_{2}})\over n_{{}_{1}}!n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma(1-n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times{1\over\Gamma(2-{D\over 2}+n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}})}\Big({p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({p^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{2-D/2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}\;,

(306)

where the intersection of their convergent regions is a nonempty proper subset of the whole parameter space.
The fact indicates that those hypergeometric functions constitute a fundamental solution
system of the GKZ-system in a proper subset of the whole parameter space.

As the integer lattice is chosen as
n_{{}_{1}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(3)}-n_{{}_{2}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(2)}

the exponents are

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}={D\over 2}-2\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}={D\over 2}-1\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}=2-{D\over 2}\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,2}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}=0,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,2}}=1-{D\over 2}\;,

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}=D-3\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,2}}=2-{D\over 2}\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}=0,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,2}}=1-{D\over 2}\;,

Applying those matrices of exponents and integer lattices,
we obtain three linear independent hypergeometric functions

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(7)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D/2-1}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D/2-2}\over(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2})(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D/2-2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{\Gamma(1-{D\over 2}+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})\Gamma({D\over 2}-2+n_{{}_{1}})\over n_{{}_{1}}!n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma({D\over 2}-1+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Gamma({D\over 2}-1+n_{{}_{2}})\Big({p^{2}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(8)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(p^{2})^{2-D/2}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{(-)^{n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}}}\Gamma(3-D+n_{{}_{1}})\Gamma({D\over 2}-1+n_{{}_{2}})\over n_{{}_{1}}!n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma(1-n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})\Gamma(3-{D\over 2}+n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Big({p^{2}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({p^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\sim{(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(p^{2})^{2-D/2}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(9)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{2-D/2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{\Gamma(2-{D\over 2}+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})\Gamma(3-D+n_{{}_{1}})\over n_{{}_{1}}!n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma(4-D+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Gamma({D\over 2}-2+n_{{}_{2}})\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({p^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}\;.

(310)

Although the intersection of the convergent regions of these series is
the empty set, the sets of the hypergeometric functions

\{\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(1)},\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(8)},\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(9)}\}

As the integer lattice is chosen as
-n_{{}_{1}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(3)}+n_{{}_{2}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(2)}
,
the matrix of exponents is

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}={D\over 2}-1\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}={D\over 2}-2\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,2}}=2-{D\over 2}\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}=1-{D\over 2},\;\alpha_{{}_{3,2}}=0\;,

(311)

whose geometric representation is plotted in Fig.
4

with

,

,

,
\{e,f\}=

\{1,6\}
. Corresponding to the integer lattice

-n_{{}_{1}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(3)}+n_{{}_{2}}E_{{}_{3}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}=D-3\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}=2-{D\over 2}\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,2}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}=1-{D\over 2},\;\alpha_{{}_{3,2}}=0\;,

(312)

whose geometric representation is presented in Fig.

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,1}}=D-3\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,2}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,1}}=0\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,2}}=2-{D\over 2}\;,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,1}}=3-D,\;\alpha_{{}_{3,2}}={D\over 2}-2\;,

(313)

Accordingly the hypergeometric series are

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(10)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D/2-2}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D/2-1}\over(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2})(p^{2})^{D/2-2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{\Gamma(1-{D\over 2}+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})\Gamma({D\over 2}-2+n_{{}_{1}})\over n_{{}_{1}}!n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma({D\over 2}-1+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Gamma({D\over 2}-1+n_{{}_{2}})\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})\over p^{2}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(11)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{2-D/2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{(-)^{n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}}}\Gamma(3-D+n_{{}_{1}})\Gamma({D\over 2}-1+n_{{}_{2}})\over n_{{}_{1}}!n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma(1-n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})\Gamma(3-{D\over 2}+n_{{}_{1}}+n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}(p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})\over p^{2}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over p^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(m_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{2-D/2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(12)}(p^{2},m_{{}_{1}}^{2},m_{{}_{2}}^{2})\sim{(m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D-3}(p^{2})^{2-D/2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-p^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\sum\limits_{n_{{}_{1}},n_{{}_{2}}}^{\infty}{\Gamma(2-{D\over 2}+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})\Gamma(3-D+n_{{}_{1}})\over n_{{}_{1}}!n_{{}_{2}}!\Gamma(4-D+n_{{}_{1}}-n_{{}_{2}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\times\Gamma({D\over 2}-2+n_{{}_{2}})\Big({p^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over m_{{}_{2}}^{2}-m_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{1}}}\Big({m_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over p^{2}}\Big)^{n_{{}_{2}}}\;.

(314)

Although the intersection of the convergent regions of these series is
the empty set, the sets of the hypergeometric functions

\{\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(3)},\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(10)},\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(11)}\}

and
\{\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(4)},\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(11)},\psi_{{}_{\{3,4,6\}}}^{(12)}\}

also constitute the fundamental solution systems of the corresponding nonempty proper subsets
of the whole parameter space, respectively.

In principle, we can embed the Feynman parametric representation of the 1-loop
self energy into infinite subvarieties of the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{3,6}}
.
However, the dimension of dual space of the GKZ-systems of most subvarieties
is much larger than the number of independent dimensionless ratios between the
square of outgoing momentum and the square of virtual masses.
Correspondingly convergent regions of the obtained hypergeometric series solutions are empty.

III
The 1-loop triangle with zero virtual masses

The Feynman integral of the 1-loop triangle diagram with zero virtual masses is represented as
the linear combination of the generalized hypergeometric functions
F_{{}_{4}}
in
literature
Davydychev1
through
\alpha
-parametric representation for the denominators.
Here, we construct the equivalent fundamental solution system through embedding
the Feynman integral on Grassmannians.

Denoting three incoming momenta as
p_{{}_{i}}

(i=1,2,3)
, we obtain the Feynman
parametric representation of the diagram as

\displaystyle C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{3}}^{2})=\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{4-D}\int{d^{D}q\over(2\pi)^{D}}{1\over q^{2}(q-p_{{}_{1}})^{2}(q+p_{{}_{2}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 68.28644pt=-i\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{4-D}{(-)^{D/2-3}\Gamma(3-{D\over 2})\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}\int_{0}^{1}dt_{{}_{1}}dt_{{}_{2}}dt_{{}_{3}}

\displaystyle\hskip 82.51282pt\times{\delta(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}+t_{{}_{3}}-1)\over(t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{2}}p_{{}_{3}}^{2}+t_{{}_{2}}t_{{}_{3}}p_{{}_{1}}^{2}+t_{{}_{1}}t_{{}_{3}}p_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{3-D/2}}\;,

(315)

where
p_{{}_{1}}+p_{{}_{2}}+p_{{}_{3}}=0
. As
p_{{}_{2}}\neq 0
,
p_{{}_{3}}\neq 0
, but
p_{{}_{2}}^{2}=p_{{}_{3}}^{2}=0
,
the Feynman parametric representation is simplified as

\displaystyle C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)=-i\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{4-D}{(-)^{D/2-3}\Gamma(3-{D\over 2})\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 82.51282pt\times\int_{0}^{1}dt_{{}_{1}}dt_{{}_{2}}t_{{}_{2}}^{D/2-3}(p_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p_{{}_{1}}^{2}t_{{}_{1}}-p_{{}_{1}}^{2}t_{{}_{2}})^{D/2-3}

\displaystyle\hskip 68.28644pt=i\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{4-D}{\Gamma(3-{D\over 2})\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 82.51282pt\times\int\omega_{{}_{3}}(t)t_{{}_{2}}^{D/2-3}t_{{}_{3}}^{3-D}(p_{{}_{1}}^{2}t_{{}_{1}}+p_{{}_{1}}^{2}t_{{}_{2}}+p_{{}_{1}}^{2}t_{{}_{3}})^{D/2-3}\;.

(316)

Eq.(
316
) can be embedded in the subvariety of the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{3,4}}

with splitting local coordinates as

\displaystyle A^{(1)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\end{array}\right)\;.

Here the first row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{1}}
,
the second row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{2}}
,
and the third row corresponds to the integration variable
t_{{}_{3}}
, respectively.
Meanwhile the first column represents the power function
t_{{}_{1}}^{0}=1
,
the second column represents the power function
t_{{}_{2}}^{D/2-3}
, and the third column
represents the power function
t_{{}_{3}}^{3-D}
,
the fourth column represents the function
p_{{}_{1}}^{2}(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}+t_{{}_{3}})
, respectively.
Taking Feynman parametric representation of the 1-loop triangle diagram
as a function of the splitting local coordinates in Eq.(
III
),

\displaystyle C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)=\Psi^{1T}(z_{{}_{1}},\;\cdots,\;z_{{}_{4}})\;,

(321)

we obviously have

\displaystyle\Psi^{1T}(\chi_{{}_{1}}z_{{}_{1}},\;\cdots,\;\chi_{{}_{4}}z_{{}_{4}})=\chi_{{}_{2}}^{D/2-3}\chi_{{}_{3}}^{3-D}\chi_{{}_{4}}^{D/2-3}\Psi^{1T}(z_{{}_{1}},\;\cdots,\;z_{{}_{4}})\;.

(322)

Where
z_{{}_{i}}\;(i=1,\cdots,4)
denotes the i-th column vector in the matrix Eq.(
III
),
and
\chi_{{}_{i}}
is a nonzero constant, respectively.
Eq.(
322
) implies that the special Feynman integral
satisfies following GKZ-system

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}+\vartheta_{{}_{1,4}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)=-C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,4}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)=-C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{3,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,4}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)=-C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)=0\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)=({D\over 2}-3)C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{3,3}}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)=(3-D)C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,4}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)=({D\over 2}-3)C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)\;.

(323)

Obviously the dual space of the GKZ-system of Eq.(
323
) is the null
space
\{0\}
, and compatible with the exponent matrix

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;-1\\
\;\;0&\;\;{D\over 2}-3&\;\;0&\;\;2-{D\over 2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;3-D&\;\;D-4\end{array}\right)\;.

Using the above matrices of exponent and integer lattice, one obtains the
unique solution of the GKZ-system as

\displaystyle C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0,\;0)={C_{{}_{1T}}\over\Gamma(3-{D\over 2})\Gamma(D-3)}\Big(p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\Big)^{D/2-3}\;,

(328)

where
C_{{}_{1T}}
is the integration constant. In the case
p_{{}_{1}}=-p_{{}_{2}}\neq 0
,

p_{{}_{3}}=0
, the Feynman parametric representation is modified as

\displaystyle C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)=-i\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{4-D}{(-)^{D/2-3}\Gamma(3-{D\over 2})\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}\int_{0}^{1}dt_{{}_{1}}dt_{{}_{2}}(1-t_{{}_{1}}-t_{{}_{2}})^{D/2-3}

\displaystyle\hskip 82.51282pt\times(t_{{}_{1}}p_{{}_{1}}^{2}+t_{{}_{1}}p_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D/2-3}

\displaystyle\hskip 68.28644pt=i\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{4-D}{\Gamma(3-{D\over 2})\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}\int\omega(t)t_{{}_{3}}^{3-D}(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}+t_{{}_{3}})^{D/2-3}

\displaystyle\hskip 82.51282pt\times(t_{{}_{1}}p_{{}_{1}}^{2}+t_{{}_{2}}p_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D/2-3}\;,

(329)

which can be embedded in the subvariety of the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{3,5}}

with splitting local coordinates as

\displaystyle A^{(1)\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;1&\;\;0\end{array}\right)\;.

In the above splitting coordinates, the Feynman integral of 1-loop massless triangle
satisfies the following GKZ-system

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}+\vartheta_{{}_{1,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{1,5}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)=-C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,5}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)=-C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{3,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,4}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)=-C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)=0\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)=0\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{3,3}}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)=(3-D)C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,4}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)=({D\over 2}-3)C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,5}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,5}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)=({D\over 2}-3)C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)\;.

(334)

Because the submatrix of Eq.(
III
) composed of the first, fourth,
fifth columns and that composed of the second, fourth,
fifth columns satisfy

\displaystyle\det A_{{}_{145}}^{(1)\prime}=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\;\;1&\;\;1&\;\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;0\\
\end{array}\right)=-p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\neq 0\;,

\displaystyle\det A_{{}_{245}}^{{(1)\prime}}=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\\
\;\;1&\;\;1&\;\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;0\\
\end{array}\right)=p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\neq 0\;,

we can choose
\{(1,0,0)^{T}
,
(1,1,1)^{T}
,
(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},p_{{}_{1}}^{2},0)^{T}\}

or
\{(0,1,0)^{T}
,
(1,1,1)^{T}
,
(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},p_{{}_{1}}^{2},0)^{T}\}
spanning
the projective plane respectively. As we choose the vectors

\{(1,0,0)^{T}
,
(1,1,1)^{T}
,
(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},p_{{}_{1}}^{2},0)^{T}\}

as the basis of the projective plane, splitting local coordinates are written as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\;\;1&\;\;-1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;1&\;\;0\\
\;\;0&\;\;{1\over p_{{}_{1}}^{2}}&\;\;-{1\over p_{{}_{1}}^{2}}&\;\;0&\;\;1\end{array}\right)\;,

with the exponent matrix

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\;\;0&\;\;-1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;2-{D\over 2}&\;\;{D\over 2}-3&\;\;0\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;1-{D\over 2}&\;\;0&\;\;{D\over 2}-3\end{array}\right)\;.

Using the above matrix of exponents and the
integer lattice
0_{{}_{3\times 5}}
, one obtains the unique solution as

\displaystyle C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)={C_{{}_{1T}}^{(145)}\over\det(A_{{}_{145}}^{(1)\prime})\Gamma(2)\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})}\Big(p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\Big)^{D/2-2}

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt=-{C_{{}_{1T}}^{(145)}\over\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})}\Big(p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\Big)^{D/2-3}\;.

(351)

Similarly taking the vectors
\{(0,1,0)^{T}
,
(1,1,1)^{T}
,
(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},p_{{}_{1}}^{2},0)^{T}\}

as the basis of the projective plane, we write the splitting local coordinates as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\;\;-1&\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;1&\;\;0\\
\;\;{1\over p_{{}_{1}}^{2}}&\;\;0&\;\;-{1\over p_{{}_{1}}^{2}}&\;\;0&\;\;1\end{array}\right)\;,

with the exponent matrix

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\;\;-1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;2-{D\over 2}&\;\;{D\over 2}-3&\;\;0\\
\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;1-{D\over 2}&\;\;0&\;\;{D\over 2}-3\end{array}\right)\;.

Using the above matrix of exponents and the
integer lattice
0_{{}_{3\times 5}}
, we write the unique solution as

\displaystyle C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;0)={C_{{}_{1T}}^{(245)}\over\det(A_{{}_{245}}^{(1)\prime})\Gamma(2)\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})}\Big(p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\Big)^{D/2-2}

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt={C_{{}_{1T}}^{(245)}\over\Gamma(2-{D\over 2})}\Big(p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\Big)^{D/2-3}\;.

(360)

III.1
The fundamental solution systems as
p_{{}_{3}}\neq 0
,
p_{{}_{3}}^{2}=0

As
p_{{}_{3}}\neq 0
,
p_{{}_{3}}^{2}=0
, the Feynman parametric representation is

\displaystyle C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)=i\Big(\Lambda_{{}_{\rm RE}}^{2}\Big)^{4-D}{\Gamma(3-{D\over 2})\over(4\pi)^{D/2}}\int\omega(t)_{{}_{3}}{t_{{}_{3}}^{3-D}(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}+t_{{}_{3}})^{D/2-3}\over(t_{{}_{2}}p_{{}_{1}}^{2}+t_{{}_{1}}p_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{3-D/2}}\;.

(361)

The integral can be embedded in the subvariety of the Grassmannian
G_{{}_{3,5}}

which local coordinates are written as

\displaystyle A^{(2)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2}&\;\;1\\
\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;p_{{}_{1}}^{2}&\;\;1\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;1&\;\;0&\;\;1\end{array}\right)\;.

Here three rows correspond to the integration variables
t_{{}_{i}}\;(i=1,\;2,\;3)
,
the first column represents the power function
t_{{}_{1}}^{0}=1
,
the second column represents the power function
t_{{}_{2}}^{0}=1
, the third column
represents the power function
t_{{}_{3}}^{3-D}
,
the fourth column represents the function
(t_{{}_{2}}p_{{}_{1}}^{2}+t_{{}_{1}}p_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D/2-3}
,
and the fifth column represents
(t_{{}_{1}}+t_{{}_{2}}+t_{{}_{3}})^{D/2-3}
, respectively.
The geometric description of the matrix is similarly presented in Fig.
1

where
(2,3,5)
is changed into
(1,2,4)
,
\{1,4\}
is changed into
\{3,5\}
,
respectively. Accordingly, the Feynman integral satisfies the following
GKZ-system in splitting local coordinates

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}+\vartheta_{{}_{1,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{1,5}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)=-C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,5}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)=-C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{3,3}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,5}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)=-C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{1,1}}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)=0\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{2,2}}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)=0\;,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{{}_{3,3}}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)=(3-D)C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,4}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,4}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)=({D\over 2}-3)C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)\;,

\displaystyle\Big\{\vartheta_{{}_{1,5}}+\vartheta_{{}_{2,5}}+\vartheta_{{}_{3,5}}\Big\}C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)=({D\over 2}-3)C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)\;.

(366)

Obviously the matrix of exponents is written as

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;\alpha_{{}_{1,4}}&\;\;\alpha_{{}_{1,5}}\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}&\;\;\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}\\
\;\;0&\;\;0&\;\;3-D&\;\;0&\;\;D-4\end{array}\right)\;,

with

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,4}}+\alpha_{{}_{1,5}}=-1,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}=-1,\;

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,4}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}={D\over 2}-3,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,5}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}=1-{D\over 2}\;.

(371)

The dual space of the GKZ-system is spanned by the integer matrix
whose submatrix composed of the fourth and fifth columns is formulated as
E_{{}_{3}}^{(3)}
,
and other elements are all zero. The basis of the dual space implies the Feynman
integral satisfying the PDE as

\displaystyle{\partial^{2}C\over\partial z_{{}_{1,4}}\partial z_{{}_{2,5}}}(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)={\partial^{2}C\over\partial z_{{}_{1,5}}\partial z_{{}_{2,4}}}(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)\;.

(372)

When the exponent matrix is given, the Feynman integral can be formally expressed as

\displaystyle C(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},\;p_{{}_{2}}^{2},\;0)=\prod\limits_{i,j}z_{{}_{i,j}}^{\alpha_{{}_{i,j}}}\varphi({p_{{}_{1}}^{2}\over p_{{}_{2}}^{2}})\;,

(373)

where
\varphi(x)
satisfies the PDE

\displaystyle x\Big[\alpha_{{}_{1,4}}\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}+(1-\alpha_{{}_{1,4}}-\alpha_{{}_{2,5}})x{d\over dx}-x^{2}{d^{2}\over dx^{2}}\Big]\varphi(x)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt=\Big[\alpha_{{}_{1,5}}\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}+(1+\alpha_{{}_{1,5}}+\alpha_{{}_{2,4}})x{d\over dx}-x^{2}{d^{2}\over dx^{2}}\Big]\varphi(x)\;.

(374)

As
\alpha_{{}_{1,5}}\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}=0
this equation recovers the well-known Gauss equation.

There are two choices on the matrix of integer lattice whose
submatrix composed of the fourth and fifth columns is formulated as
\pm nE_{{}_{3}}^{(3)}
with
n\geq 0
.
The matrix of integer lattice
nE_{{}_{3}}^{(3)}
permits two
choices on the exponents. The first one is

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,4}}=0,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,5}}=-1,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}={D\over 2}-3,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}=2-{D\over 2},

(375)

whose geometric representation is plotted in Fig.
2
(2)
with
(a,b)=

(2,4)
,
\{c,d,e\}=

\{1,3,5\}
. The second choice
of exponents is

\displaystyle\alpha_{{}_{1,4}}={D\over 2}-2,\;\alpha_{{}_{1,5}}=1-{D\over 2},\;\alpha_{{}_{2,4}}=-1,\;\alpha_{{}_{2,5}}=0,

(376)

whose geometric description is presented in Fig.
2
(1) with

a=1
,
\{(b,c)
,
(d,e)\}=

\{(2,4)
,
(3,5)\}
, respectively.
The corresponding hypergeometric functions are evidently written as

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{1,2,3\}}}^{(1)}(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},p_{{}_{2}}^{2},0)\sim(p_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D/2-3}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big({p_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over p_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big)^{n}\sim{(p_{{}_{1}}^{2})^{D/2-2}\over p_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\;,

\displaystyle\psi_{{}_{\{1,2,3\}}}^{(2)}(p_{{}_{1}}^{2},p_{{}_{2}}^{2},0)\sim{(p_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D/2-2}\over p_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big({p_{{}_{2}}^{2}\over p_{{}_{1}}^{2}}\Big)^{n}\sim{(p_{{}_{2}}^{2})^{D/2-2}\over p_{{}_{1}}^{2}-p_{{}_{2}}^{2}}\;,

(377)

where the intersection of their convergent regions is
|p_{{}_{2}}^{2}/p_{{}_{1}}^{2}|\leq 1
.
The linearly independent Gauss functions constitute the fundamental
solution system of the GKZ-system of Eq.(
366
) in the region
|p_{{}_{2}}^{2}/p_{{}_{1}}^{2}|\leq 1

which is consistent with the results in the literature. As
\mu=\nu=\rho=1

and
k^{2}=0
in Eq.(7) of Ref.
Davydychev1
, the fourth type Appell functions of the second
and third terms are modified into the Gauss functions in Eq.(
377
).
